## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 1:**
- Champion (16884.88): 2-state FSM. State 0 = Exploration (rwm=24), transitions to state 1 when it sees blue (color 3) with p=0.39. State 1 = ZigZag (zfr=62, cyan LED), with 4 transitions including "below threshold neighbors" and "gray floor" back to state 0.
- 2nd place (18660.41): 4-state with FlockCenter, Stop, Repulsion - significantly worse.
- Large gap between 1st (16884) and 2nd (18660) suggests the champion has a notably good structure.

**Mission understanding:** AGGREGATION - robots should cluster together. Score increases by 1 for each robot outside the target region... wait, re-reading: "score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region." The target region is the BLUE-bordered strip. So robots need to get to the BLACK-floored strip near BLUE walls.

Wait - actually for AGGREGATION the score depends on robots being in the target region. The floor is BLACK in both strips. The target is the blue-bordered strip. Robots need to navigate to the blue strip.

The champion works by: exploring until seeing blue (color 3 = blue walls), then zigzagging. The zigzag with transitions back to exploration when few neighbors or on gray floor makes sense.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit: small tweaks to champion topology
3. Explore: different approaches - GoToColor blue directly, attraction-based clustering, etc.

**Machines to generate:**

1. **Champion (exact copy)** - survival

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 2:**

Huge improvement: champion went from 16884 to 6598. The winning machine is the 4-state FSM I proposed as an exploration candidate.

**Champion structure analysis:**
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=24) → transitions to State 2 when sees blue (p=0.39) OR on black floor (p=0.85)
- State 1: Attraction (att=2.5) → transitions to State 0 when few neighbors (below 8, steep) OR State 2 when on gray (p=0.35)
- State 2: ZigZag (zfr=62) → 4 transitions back to State 1 (below 10 neighbors, below 2 neighbors, random 0.63, gray 0.48)
- State 3: GoToColor blue (vel=1.0) → transitions to State 2 when on black (p=0.75) OR State 0 with p=0.03

Key insight: The robot explores, goes to blue when it sees it (state 3), then zigzags in the blue region (state 2), with attraction behavior (state 1) pulling robots together.

**2nd place (8215):** Simple 2-state: Explore → GoToColor(blue), with transitions back when few neighbors/gray/random. Much simpler but decent.

**Gap analysis:** 6598 vs 8215 is a significant gap. The 4-state structure is clearly superior. 3rd-14th are much worse.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit champion: tweak parameters (att gain, rwm, zfr, probabilities, thresholds)
3. Combine champion with 2nd place insights
4. Explore: variations on the 4-state topology

Key parameters to tune:
- State 0: rwm (24→15-35), black floor prob (0.85→0.7-0.95)
- State 1: att gain (2.5→1.5-4.0), transition thresholds
- State 2: zfr (62→40-80), transition probabilities
- State 3: black floor prob (0.75→0.6-0.9)

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 3:**

The scores are tightly clustered (5898-7290 for top 12), suggesting the topology is well-established and we're doing fine-tuning. The champion improved from 6598 to 5898.

**Champion structure:**
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=20) → State 2 if sees blue (p=0.39) OR State 2 if on black (p=0.85)
- State 1: Attraction (att=2.0) → State 0 if few neighbors (<8, steep) OR State 2 if on gray (p=0.3)
- State 2: ZigZag (zfr=62) → State 1 if <10 neighbors | random 0.6 | <2 neighbors | gray 0.45
- State 3: GoToColor blue → State 2 if on black (p=0.8) OR State 0 with p=0.03

**Key observations:**
- rwm=20 beats rwm=24 (lower turning rate helps navigation)
- att=2.0 beats att=2.5 (less aggressive attraction)
- p0x1=0.85 (black floor → state 2) is consistently good
- The 2nd best (6323) added an extra transition from state 0: also sees blue with p=0.5 → state 2 (redundant but helps)
- The GoToColor-in-state-2 variant (rank 7, 6687) also performed well
- State 3 transitions: black→state2 (p=0.8) + random (p=0.03)

**Parameters to tune for exploitation:**
- rwm: try 15, 18, 22, 25
- att: try 1.5, 1.8, 2.2
- zfr: try 50, 55, 62, 70
- p0x1 (black→state2): try 0.80, 0.85, 0.90, 0.95
- p3x0 (black→state2 from state3): try 0.75, 0.80, 0.85, 0.90
- p1x1 (gray→state2 from state1): try 0.25, 0.3, 0.35, 0.40
- Add transition from state 0: also goes to state 3 when sees blue

**Exploration ideas:**
- Try state 1 as FlockCenter instead of Attraction
- Add transition from state 2 back to state 3 (GoToColor) when sees blue
- Try state 3 with additional transitions

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 6:**

Champion improved to 5286 (rwm=8). Clear trend: lower rwm = better (8 > 10 > 14 > 18).

Key observations:
- rwm=8 beats rwm=10 (5286 vs 5539), both clearly better than rwm=14
- The trend strongly suggests trying rwm=5, 6, 7 (even lower)
- p0x0=0.39 (sees blue→zigzag) is good; p0x0=0.35 beats 0.39 at rwm=14 (rank 3 vs 4) - interesting
- p0x1=0.85 (black→zigzag) consistently good
- att=2.0 beats att=1.5 (rank 9)
- p2x1=0.6, p2x3=0.45 are champion values and consistently beat alternatives
- p3x0=0.8 beats p3x0=0.85 (rank 1 vs 12 with p3x0=0.85)
- Adding extra transitions to state 3 hurts (rank 13)
- p1x1=0.25 barely worse than 0.30 (rank 7 vs champion structure)
- zfr=62 seems optimal; 55, 70 tied with it at rwm=14 but champion uses 62

**Strategy for Round 7:**
1. Keep champion (rwm=8)
2. Try rwm=5, 6, 7 - pushing lower
3. Try rwm=4, 3 (minimum is 1)
4. Combine rwm=8 with p0x0=0.35 (worked well at rwm=14)
5. Try rwm=8 with p0x1=0.90 
6. Try rwm=8 with att=1.8
7. Keep some rwm=10 variants as backup

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 8:**

Champion held at 4420.82 (rwm=3). Round 8's best was 4428 (rwm=4) - very close but not better. The scores are now extremely tight (4420-4910 range).

**Key findings:**
- rwm=4 (4428) ≈ rwm=3 (4420) - essentially tied, stochastic noise
- rwm=3 with p0x0=0.42: 4464 (rank 2) - slightly worse than 0.39
- rwm=3 with p0x1=0.80: 4477 (rank 3) - slightly worse than 0.85
- rwm=3 with p3x1=0.04: 4583 (rank 4) - worse than 0.03
- rwm=3 with p3x0=0.85, p3x1=0.02: 4644 (rank 5) - slightly worse
- rwm=2 with p3x0=0.85: 4657 (rank 6)
- rwm=3 with p3x0=0.75: 4693 (rank 7) - worse than 0.80
- rwm=3 with p2x3=0.50: 4720 (rank 8) - worse than 0.45
- rwm=1 (with p2x3=0.50): 4910 (rank 13)

The champion parameters are very well-tuned. The scores 4760x3 suggest many variants are essentially equivalent.

**What hasn't been tried:**

- p2x0 threshold: 10 (always used), try 9 or 8
- w2x0: 11.4599 (always used), try 10 or 13
- w2x2: 9.5709 (always used), try 8 or 11
- Different zfr: always 62, try 60, 65
- att1=1.8 with rwm=3
- p1x0 threshold: 8 (always), try 7 or 9
- Adding extra transitions from state 2 (5th condition)

4. Try p0x1=0.82, 0.83 (between 0.80 champion and 0.85)

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
